=== PAGE 106 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 106 
 
 
8. 
The environmental and social safeguards category of the project is category B, and the 
accredited entity (AE) has submitted relevant documentation (environmental and social 
management framework) as part of the funding proposal package. 
2.2 
Component-by-component analysis 
Component 1. Sustainable development and rehabilitation of perimeters vulnerable to the adverse 
effects of climate change and reducing greenhouse gas emissions (total cost: EUR 31,401,768;  
GCF cost: EUR 20,944,116)  
9. 
With the decrease in rainfall and looming food insecurity, there is a need to increase the 
irrigation areas in Niger to achieve food security. Component 1 aims to develop new irrigation 
areas as well as rehabilitate the existing irrigation schemes, known as Aménagement Hydro 
Agricole (AHA) (hydro-agriculture development). The existing AHAs have been developed in 
Niger with inadequate irrigation networks, which results in inefficient use of water, and uses 
diesel generators and the fossil fuel-powered public electricity network to pump irrigation 
water, thereby emitting greenhouse gases (GHGs). The project interventions have been 
designed to adopt climate-resilient practices, such as using solar pumping for irrigation in 23 
areas covering 1,001 ha and rehabilitating the two existing AHAs, spanning an area of 759 ha. In 
addition, efficient irrigation techniques such as drip irrigation will be deployed to enhance 
water use efficiency thus allowing additional areas to be brought under irrigation. 
10. 
Furthermore, the AHAs will be developed along with other climate-resilient practices to 
avoid the adverse effects of extreme climatic events such as flood and drought. The adoption of 
drip irrigation will provide total control over the water flow while the farming plots will be 
protected by constructing erosion-control structures, planting trees and other flood control 
measures. 
11. 
The component includes installation of solar energy for pumping irrigation water from 
bore holes, replacing the traditional diesel generators and thus contributing to GHG emission 
reductions. Complete sets of installation kits will be provided, with each being customizable to 
suit the site-specific characteristics. The solar-powered pumping systems are primarily meant 
for irrigation services, but an additional 37 solar pumping systems will be installed to provide 
drinking water to livestock across the project sites. 
12. 
The activities will capitalize on the institutional mechanisms developed by the 
Government of Niger for AHAs, such as determination of the perimeter (i.e. installing new or 
repairing and rehabilitating boundary fences, ditches and so on), formation of farmer’s 
cooperatives and management of water resources. Inclusion of poor people, groups that are 
most vulnerable to climate change and smallholder farmers will be ensured through the use of a 
comprehensive set of eligibility criteria for selecting participant farmers/producers. 
13. 
This component comprehensively addresses the challenges of developing and expanding 
irrigated agriculture, which are currently being exacerbated by extreme weather events.  
Component 2: Support of the sustainable increase in agricultural productivity and income of 
farmers’ groups and cooperatives in irrigated areas on developed and rehabilitated perimeters 
(total cost: EUR 3,288,941; GCF cost: EUR 1,113,536)  
14. 
Under this component, farmers’ cooperatives will be formed to delineate the perimeters 
and optimal development of irrigated areas. Plot allocation to beneficiaries will be undertaken 
based on established methodologies, best practice and inclusive processes. The farmers will be 
supported so that they can establish and manage cooperatives, and operations will be 
undertaken through contracts between cooperatives and the relevant government entities. 
15. 
Improved crop production technologies will be promoted to increase productivity and 
income of farming communities. Climate-resilient practices will be adopted for crops selected

=== PAGE 107 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 107 
 
 
on the basis of local preferences and suitability to the local environment. Site-level planning will 
be undertaken based on agro-climatological information to facilitate informed decision-making. 
Provision will be made for acquiring farming equipment, inputs such as improved seeds 
including drought resistant varieties, manures, among others. The operation and maintenance 
of irrigation and solar energy infrastructure will be ensured through development and 
implementation of a robust operation and maintenance plan. Community training programmes 
will be implemented and farmers’ cooperatives will be involved to ensure effective operation 
and long-term maintenance of the water and energy infrastructure to be 
constructed/rehabilitated by the project. 
16. 
Market access will be enhanced through the development of climate-resilient value 
chains and interventions in the areas of business plan development, promotion of income-
generating activities including agricultural processing, and construction of warehouses that will 
enhance market linkages and profitability of farming communities.   
Component 3: Stakeholders’ technical, organizational and financial capacities development for the 
climate-resilient agricultural practices promotion (total cost: EUR 9,370,757; GCF cost: EUR 
7,551,205)  
17. 
The capacities of all stakeholders, including producers, market intermediaries and 
financial institutions, will be developed to implement climate-resilient practices in agricultural 
production and across the entire value chain. Farmers will be trained on climate-smart 
technologies, organizational management of cooperatives and financial management. 
18. 
Access to finance in the agriculture sector will be improved through the project 
interventions. A credit facility to be named the Agricultural Loan Facility will be established at 
Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD), the AE, which will on-lend to selected 
financial institutions that in turn will lend to the end beneficiaries on concessional terms and 
conditions. The Agricultural Loan Facility will serve as an incentive to crowd-in private sector 
investment in agriculture value chains through simplified procedures. Provisions will be made 
to ensure that the most needy farmers and cooperatives could avail the financing from the 
facility. A range of training activities will be undertaken to strengthen the capacities of banks 
and microfinance institutions regarding due diligence, design of financial products and 
monitoring to promote investments in climate-resilient agricultural lending. The facility will 
serve as an innovative model for replication throughout the country. 
19. 
In addition, a clear set of activities has been planned to ensure monitoring of results, 
derive lessons learned and share knowledge. The best practices will be disseminated through 
various means and will serve as tools for informed policy decisions in expanding climate 
resilience across other AHAs in the country.  
III. 
Assessment of performance against investment criteria  
3.1 
Impact potential                                                          Scale: Medium to high 
20. 
As the proposal is cross-cutting, it is expected to bring both adaptation and mitigation 
benefits, although the share of adaptation is relatively higher. In terms of adaptation 
beneficiaries, the number of people expected to directly benefit from the project activities is 
121,615 people. This beneficiary calculation derives from a range of project activities: 
producers’ households on the newly irrigated AHAs (56,056 people); producers’ households on 
the rehabilitated AHAs (23,415); beneficiaries of the line of credit for scaling up through the 
private finance sector (12,320); groups of small-scale farmers and others benefiting from 
income-generating activities (14,000); state workers who would benefit from capacity-building

=== PAGE 108 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 108 
 
 
activities (500); and people directly acquiring agricultural products from project sites to 
support their food security (15,324). 
21. 
The two largest sets of beneficiaries above correspond to project component 1, the 
rehabilitation and development of AHAs. The project aims to rehabilitate 749 ha of agricultural 
land and develop 1,000 ha of new AHAs. This new and rehabilitated land will sustainably 
increase the availability of food both among producers and at the national level. 
22. 
Indirect beneficiaries total 1 million, based on population estimates in the villages 
benefiting from the project. The indirect beneficiaries in these villages will benefit from 
dissemination of good agricultural practices through guides and dissemination of agro-
meteorological information, among other things.  
23. 
On the mitigation side, the proposed project is estimated to reduce or avoid 227,551 
tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) over its lifespan, which corresponds to an annual 
figure of approximately 11,176 tCO2eq. The Secretariat has verified the calculations and, while 
the mitigation impact figures are relatively modest, they still represent meaningful expected 
impact on the mitigation side of the project.  
3.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
                                  Scale: Medium to high 
24. 
As noted in the proposal document, the proposed project will be the first of its kind on 
AHAs in Niger. The ability to replicate and scale up the project across Niger will depend at least 
in part on its neutral or positive impact on water resources; the analysis as provided indicates 
that scaling up by a factor 50 to 100 is feasible from a water resource perspective. Further 
upscaling of the project in the future is feasible and here the risk of overexploitation of the 
resource and therefore maladaptation is also limited. While the AE has stated that the project 
could be scaled up across the West African region and beyond, this statement would need to be 
assessed more thoroughly in the context of a future project. 
25. 
In terms of innovation, the use of solar energy for irrigation on hydro-agricultural 
developments has not yet expanded into Niger. The technical institutions involved in the project 
have previously piloted AHA development activities from thermal power generators or 
electrical power from the national grid, but clean energy remains relatively new and innovative 
in the context of AHAs. Potential beneficiaries of the irrigation areas to be developed lack the 
technical and organizational capacity to implement the project's activities on the sites, and will 
receive the institutional and technical capacity-building needed. The planned interventions are 
based on government initiatives and promote innovative approaches and technology transfer. 
26. 
In terms of paradigm shift on the financing side, it is expected that GCF’s interventions 
will significantly reduce interest rates to small farmer borrowers from 10–20 per cent to around 
7–8 per cent in the long term. By making the loan rates more affordable, the loans will gradually 
become more attractive once the project has ended, thus stimulating more loan applications and 
investment in resilient agriculture. 
27. 
By demonstrating the potential of the interventions and making financing more 
affordable, future cooperatives and agricultural groups should be more willing to take out a 
bank loan adapted to develop their irrigation areas. This approach, if extended to other farms by 
the ministry responsible for agriculture and also supported by this project’s loan facility or a 
similar financial mechanism, will decrease reliance on grant funding and support agricultural 
groups and cooperatives to transition to other sources of private financing. 
3.3 
Sustainable development potential                        Scale: Medium to high

=== PAGE 109 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 109 
 
 
28. 
Sustainable development potential is particularly strong regarding livelihoods and 
economic co-benefits. The project is expected to provide stable incomes for beneficiaries, 
especially young people who often migrate elsewhere due to lack of economic opportunity. The 
project will therefore limit to some extent the phenomenon of rural exodus seen in the project 
area. 
29. 
Other notable co-benefits include: a significant estimated improvement in production 
and yields of between 21 per cent and 78 per cent; the creation of new income streams as the 
resources coming from agroforestry income, water savings, and clean energy savings, which can 
represent 198 per cent of agriculture production income; and a significant economic boost to 
agricultural groups and cooperatives.  
3.4 
Needs of the recipient                                                                   Scale: High 
30. 
As noted in the project documents and in other GCF projects in the country, Niger is a 
least developed country and faces extraordinary challenges in ensuring its development aligns 
with climate-resilient, low-emission development pathways. Agriculture is the main source of 
income for about 87 per cent of the population, and the sector is severely impacted and 
threatened by the adverse effects of climate change, including irregular rainfall, shorter rainy 
seasons, droughts, flooding and lack of water availability for cropping beyond the rainy season. 
31. 
At the local level, project beneficiaries are vulnerable farmers and their groups and 
cooperatives. These farmers tend to be small producers of wheat, maize, rice and vegetables. 
Soil poverty, low levels of irrigation and small or marginal agricultural areas increase the 
climate vulnerability of producers and limit their ability to adapt to climate change-related 
risks.   
3.5 
Country ownership 
                                                                  Scale: High 
32. 
The project will contribute to the implementation of the 2017–2020 Four-Year Contract-
Plan between the Niger State and the project’s executing entity (EE), Niger’s Office of Hydro-
Agricultural Development (ONAHA). Niger considers ONAHA to be central to its AHA strategy, 
which it sees as critical to food security. This four-year contract-plan targets 4,000 ha of new 
hydro-agricultural developments and 2,000 ha of rehabilitated hydro-agricultural 
developments. 
33. 
The project is consistent with Niger’s nationally determined contribution. The 
development of AHAs with climate-resilient techniques and a solar water pumping system is in 
line with the co-benefit actions of adaptation and mitigation advocated by the nationally 
determined contribution. The project will also contribute to national priorities for the 
agriculture, forestry and other land use (AFOLU) sector.  
3.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
                                  Scale: Medium to high 
34. 
The profitability of the developed irrigation areas (see the funding proposal: feasibility 
study, and economic and financial analysis) shows that producers, in the short term, will be able 
to bear the costs of replacing irrigation systems with a lifespan of 5–10 years.   
35. 
The project aims to promote solar energy instead of thermal generators for water 
pumping. The California irrigation system and the drip irrigation system are promoted in place 
of the open-channel irrigation system. These promoted systems have a return of at least 85 per 
cent compared with about 56 per cent for open-channel systems. In the latter case, the losses 
are high, and the maintenance costs are significant. To ensure the quality and performance of

=== PAGE 110 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 110 
 
 
the equipment to be acquired as part of the project, the contractor must commit to providing 
equipment that will have a lifespan of at least 10 and 25 years, respectively, for the pumps and 
solar panels. 
IV. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies 
4.1 
Environmental and social safeguards 
36. 
Project background. The project aims to develop AHAs with climate-smart agriculture 
techniques to ensure adaptation of agricultural populations to the adverse effects of climate 
change, mitigation of GHGs and improvement of production yields and incomes of farmers’ 
groups and cooperatives. The project has three components: (1) sustainable development and 
rehabilitation of perimeters vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change; (2) support for 
the development of new and rehabilitated perimeters; and (3) developing stakeholders’ 
technical and organizational capabilities to promote climate-resilient agricultural practices. The 
environmental co-benefits of the project include sustainable management of water resources;  
reducing fossil fuel consumption and GHG emissions;  improving or maintaining soil quality and 
maintaining biodiversity. The social co-benefits include better access to energy for irrigation;  
improving the food security of beneficiaries;  promoting sustainable agricultural development;  
improved health and safety;  improved access to education; and reducing the phenomenon of 
rural exodus. 
37. 
Environmental and social risk category. The project is classified as category B, 
because it is not expected to negatively affect forest resources or indigenous populations. The 
AE also indicated that the project will not result to involuntary resettlement of populations. 
Project activities will not take place in disputed areas, or areas sensitive to land issues or in 
natural reserves. The Secretariat confirms the environmental and social (E&S) risk classification 
and is within the AE’s accreditation level.  
38. 
Safeguard instruments. The AE’s environmental and social performance standards are 
applied to the project under the larger framework of Niger’s Law on the Environment, which 
requires any development activity that could potentially harm the environment to go through 
an environmental and social assessment. For the project, The AE has prepared an ESMF. An 
integrated pest and pesticide management plan (IPPMP) has also been developed to ensure the 
rational use of chemical products such as pesticides and agro-chemicals when implementing the 
activities and to promote integrated pest management. 
39. 
Compliance with the GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards. 
Paragraphs 40–47 below summarize the project’s compliance with the GCF ESS standards (ESS 
1–8). 
40. 
ESS 1: Assessment and management of E&S risks and impacts. The ESMF provides 
an overview of the potential E&S risks and impacts that the project may have. Each of the sites 
selected under the project (i.e. 23 new sites as well as rehabilitation of two old sites) constitutes 
the physical framework of a subproject that will be the subject of an environmental and social 
impact assessment (ESIA). Each site constitutes a hydro-agricultural development that will be 
assessed from an environmental and social perspective in accordance with the country’s 
regulations. Each of the subprojects will undergo screening, scoping, public consultation 
including approval of the ESIA, and acquisition of a certificate of environmental compliance. 
41. 
ESS 2: Labour and working conditions. The AE has determined that the risk factors 
associated with ESS 2 are discrimination and marginalization of certain groups in employment, 
child employment and forced labour. Discrimination and marginalization risk factors relate to 
Niger companies’ pattern of hiring only able-bodied men and young men who can perform

=== PAGE 111 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 111 
 
 
difficult manual work. Women and people with disabilities are thus routinely considered unfit 
and are seldom hired. The AE has made a commitment to complying with Niger’s Labour Code, 
which prohibits forced or compulsory labour. To further mitigate these risks, the AE will 
establish criteria for fair hiring practices; for sensitizing parents about the prohibition of child 
labour; and for respecting provisions of the Labour Code and the International Labour 
Organization conventions. 
42. 
ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention. Identified potential risks and 
impacts include pressure on ground water, surface groundwater pollution, change in soil 
structure, loss of arable land and air pollution. Each of these risks/impacts is addressed in the 
ESMF via mitigation/avoidance and enhancement measures during both the construction and 
operational phases. The ESMF also notes that the choice of solar energy represents an 
environmentally efficient alternative to thermal energy. As indicated above, there is also an 
IPPMP to guide the management of pests and use of pesticides and agro-chemicals. Each of the 
potential risks and impacts will be further identified while conducting the ESIA and an 
environmental and social management plan will be developed in accordance with the E&S 
performance standards. Mitigation and prevention measures will be determined commensurate 
with the level of impacts and risks as identified in the field. 
43. 
ESS 4: Community health, safety and security. The potential risks and negative 
impacts as identified under the project include potential security issues such as theft of 
technical equipment and inadequate maintenance of technical equipment, a range of health and 
safety impacts, and traffic disruption. Health and safety impacts may include the absence of 
adequate personal protective equipment, which could expose workers to injuries and 
respiratory illnesses. In addition, the influx of migrant workers from other regions might result 
in the rise in transmittable diseases, ranging from COVID-19 to HIV-AIDS. The mitigation 
measures described in the ESMF include identifying risks that may arise during both 
construction and operational phases, and informing workers and staff to the identified risks and 
monitoring the use of personal protective equipment (including use of first-aid kits). The project 
will also include measures to raise awareness among communities of various health and safety 
risks by conducting environment, health and safety awareness activities. 
44. 
ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. As indicated in the funding 
proposal, the AE has determined that ESS 5 is not applicable under the project, because it will 
not involve involuntary resettlement of populations. Land acquisition will be on a voluntary (e.g. 
donation) basis and any person who is not interested in the project will not be forced to 
participate. The project has likewise developed a “land securing methodology” for developing 
and replicating the AHA concept. Only those farmers who choose to participate in the scheme 
will be part of the process of land title establishment, which will be done in a participatory 
manner.  
45. 
ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural 
resources. The ESMF includes descriptions of the biophysical characteristics of each of the five 
regions identified in the project, several of which include nationally protected grasslands. The 
large water supplies in the arid or semi-arid areas of the project may create local microclimates 
that are conducive to proliferation of invasive plants – at the irrigated perimeters as well as 
downstream along drainage canals. The project could result in loss of vegetation cover, 
landscape modifications, and disturbance or destruction of wildlife habitats and poaching. A 
series of measures to avoid and mitigate these risks are briefly listed in the ESMF, including 
requirements such as limiting tree-felling, paying tree-felling taxes, redistributing cut wood to 
communities and carrying out reforestation in project areas. The AE has also made a 
commitment to “offsetting losses of cash crops and trees with economic value”. Regarding 
disturbance and/or destruction of wildlife habitat and poaching, mitigation efforts are to 
include marking out the rights-of-way to avoid unnecessary overflow, especially when opening

=== PAGE 112 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 112 
 
 
new access roads to the sites, and prohibiting raiding and poaching practices by staff and 
workers. 
46. 
ESS 7: Indigenous peoples and the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. The AE has 
confirmed that the project sites are not in the nomadic Tuareg areas, and any herders that could 
be in the project area are sedentary. The AE has included in the ESMF the provision that if any 
indigenous people are found in the project area, taking into consideration the GCF Indigenous 
Peoples Policy scope of application, an indigenous peoples plan will be developed and their free, 
prior and informed consent will be sought before any subproject is approved, in accordance 
with the requirements of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. 
47. 
ESS 8: Cultural heritage. The project activities may include “site development work (i.e. 
clearing, parcelling, construction of pipeline structures, etc.)” and “opening of site access roads” 
– hence, there is the possibility of uncovering objects of cultural value. Should the discovery of 
physical cultural items occur, this will be reported to the relevant government authority and the 
AE for appropriate action. 
48. 
Implementation arrangements. The project will establish a project management unit 
(PMU) to coordinate the implementation of the environmental and social management 
measures, as well as capacity-building and training activities. ESS expertise will also be 
established at the PMU to implement and monitor those measures in close coordination with 
relevant agencies. Implementation of site-specific environmental and social management plans 
will be undertaken by contractors and service providers who will be required to have 
environment, health and safety personnel.  
49. 
Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure. Public consultations were 
undertaken with various categories of actors. These included consultations with technical 
services stakeholders at the central and regional level, consultations with beneficiary 
communities at the local level, and two national workshops to validate draft study reports. 
Technical consultations included meetings with representatives from the various government 
ministries. Community consultations were held with villagers in the five project regions of 
Agadez, Diffa, Maradi, Tahoua and Zinder. Key concerns and stakeholders’ suggestions and 
recommendations for addressing them were raised in these meetings. During the conduct of the 
ESIA for each of the subprojects, a stakeholder engagement plan to be approved by the PMU will 
also be prepared. 
50. 
Grievance redress mechanism. The ESMF provides for management of grievances first 
at the local level, then elevated to the level of the AE or the GCF if local resolution is not possible. 
At the local level, grievance management will be based primarily on existing practices that rely 
on the populations’ general preference for conciliation with customary leaders (village and 
canton chiefs). Grievances will be collected via a publicly available notebook in each community 
participating in a subproject. The public will be informed about the existence of the book, and it 
will be accessible to interested parties, including non-government organizations. The project 
proposes the following approach in an effort to amicably resolve conflicts that may arise: first 
level of resolution is provided by the village chief; in the second level, in the event of failure to 
achieve a resolution, the issue will be escalated to the mayor of the community; and at the third 
level, in the event of an impasse at the first two levels, the dispute will be addressed by the 
administrative authority. 
4.2 
Gender policy  
51. 
The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and 
therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.  
52. 
The gender assessment indicates the existence of an enabling environment for 
promoting gender equality in Niger. The country has a national gender policy, a constitution and

=== PAGE 113 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 113 
 
 
laws that should contribute to gender equality. In addition to the legislative framework, Niger 
has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All forms of Discrimination against Women 
(CEDAW). The institutional framework consists of a national ministry for the promotion of 
women and child protection, which is supported by decentralized services of gender cells and 
focal points within sectoral ministries. Other institutional mechanisms include the directorate 
for the enrolment of girls, which is responsible for implementing the government’s policy on the 
enrolment of girls into schools, and the national observatory for gender promotion which 
monitors gender equity and equality issues. Nonetheless, some legal codes still present barriers 
for women’s access to opportunities. For example, the civil code limits women’s capacity to 
follow professions and open bank accounts. The gender assessment discusses the established 
norms, cultural and religious aspects that dictate gender dynamics and that pose challenges for 
women to attain equality. Social norms still govern gender issues and men are viewed as both 
breadwinners and decision-makers with women depending on them. 
53. 
The gender assessment is based on a review of literature available to the AE. The AE has 
not yet undertaken stakeholder discussions and has not solicited inputs from stakeholders 
during the preparation of the assessment. The context of gender issues described in the gender 
assessment is focused on the national level, with very limited information on areas where the 
project will be implemented, although the village sites have been identified. The AE has 
committed to carry out further site-specific gender analyses involving relevant and various 
stakeholders.  
54. 
The gender assessment provided discusses gender-related matters including on 
employment, education and access to information among others. Women are more likely to be 
victims of gender-based violence of various forms. The gender assessment indicates that there 
are mechanisms for preventing gender-based violence and providing remedial actions, 
including the project’s grievance mechanism, although the latter have not been described in 
detail. The project mechanism that will be available to stakeholders who have concerns on the 
project is described in the environmental and social management framework, although the 
gender assessment does not include information on how it will be made accessible to both 
women and men, given the prevailing cultural/traditional norms and practices in Niger. 
55. 
Women constitute more than 70 per cent percent of the national workforce in the 
agricultural sector. In this sector women have lower productivity levels than men and 
encounter barriers such as limited access to manual labour, irrigation water, finance and 
fertilizers, and participation in leadership positions outside the household set-up. Additionally, 
access to land is mainly through inheritance and is generally reserved for men. This in turn 
negatively impacts the resilience of women to climate change effects: consequently, female-
headed households are less resilient than male-headed households and they are more food 
insecure. Issues that are likely to affect and render women more vulnerable in the context of the 
project have been identified as low literacy and incomes; local cultural traditions; barriers to 
accessing land; and limited involvement in decision-making processes. However, some of the 
mitigation measures proposed are not well integrated into the gender action plan. 
56. 
The AE has provided a project-level gender action plan that includes activities, 
performance indicators, baseline and targets, budgets, timelines and responsible parties. 
Budgetary allocations are included in the action plan for most activities while for others 
financial resources for implementation have been incorporated into the project’s budget. The 
action plan includes an activity to undertake additional gender analyses for the 25 project sites. 
The analyses are expected to address the gap seen in stakeholder consultations, identify gender-
related actions and carry out a baseline survey prior to commencing implementation of the 
project.  
57. 
Activities listed in the gender action plan reflect opportunities presented by the project 
to provide access to solar-powered irrigation water, farming implements such as fertilizers, 
climate information, and participation in training on climate-smart agriculture and decision-

=== PAGE 114 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 114 
 
 
making in farmers’ groups and cooperatives by both women and men. Furthermore, the project 
will train women and young beneficiaries of the project on the maintenance of equipment that 
will be installed by the project and will provide capacity-building for income-generating 
activities. The project will promote fair allocation of land and AHAs. This is important given the 
structural gender-related issues of land ownership faced by women. Targets include female-
headed households for activities such as access to land and agricultural implements and 
participation in decision-making bodies – there is room to add similar targets for other activities 
and indicators. However, some opportunities presented by the project to ensure both women 
and men benefit have not been incorporated in the action plan. For example, the project 
includes an agricultural loan facility that could improve women’s access to finance or credit to 
address their lower income levels, which hamper agricultural productivity as indicated in the 
assessment. 
58. 
Implementation arrangements include having a gender expert in the project 
management unit whose responsibilities include mainstreaming gender issues in all relevant 
processes, and implementing, supervising and monitoring the execution of the action plan.  
59. 
The AE is advised to revise the targets in the gender action plan based on the further 
gender and baseline analyses that are planned. The AE is recommended to include more targets 
in the gender action plan for female-headed households considering that the gender assessment 
pointed out that they are less resilient to the effects of climate change than male-headed 
households and also constitute 16 per cent of Nigerien households. For instance, targets for this 
group can be considered for access to solar irrigation water, finance, credit and training offered 
by the project for improving their resilience to climate change. Furthermore, outcomes related 
to behavioural change resulting from access to project benefits can also be included in the 
action plan. In addition, additional gender analyses should be accompanied by inputs from 
stakeholder engagement of potential beneficiaries with any necessary changes reflected in the 
gender action plan. 
4.3 
Risks  
4.3.1. 
Overall project assessment (medium risk) 
60. 
The funding proposal aims to increase agricultural production by improving crop yields 
through AHAs and strengthened stakeholders’ technological and financial capacities. GCF is 
requested to provide a grant of EUR 24.2 million and a loan of EUR 5.89 million. The borrowers 
of the GCF loan are local financial institutions to be selected by the AE based on its due diligence. 
The AE is providing a sovereign loan of EUR 9.2 million and the Government of Niger is waiving 
taxes worth of EUR 6.1 million as co-financing for the project.  
61. 
Niger is a least developed country. The project includes activities with revenue-
generating potential that will be financed by GCF grants, a GCF loan and the AE’s sovereign loan. 
The AE clarified that the GCF grant and BOAD loan will be used by the Government of Niger for 
the same outputs to support the overall bankability of the project while reducing the debt 
burden of the country.  
4.3.2. 
Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project 
(medium risk) 
62. 
BOAD, as a direct access AE, has been operating in the West African region for 40 years 
and has experience in financing climate change projects in eight countries of the West African 
Economic and Monetary Union (UEMOA) including Niger. The AE will be directly executing the 
loan facility under activity 3.3.2.

=== PAGE 115 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 115 
 
 
63. 
ONAHA will be EE of this project and will be responsible for all project activities apart 
from the loan facility. The EE has a track record of implementing projects financed by external 
donor funding and the average size of the budget is under EUR 2 million. BOAD has carried out a 
capacity assessment of ONAHA, and found the results to be satisfactory. 
4.3.3. 
Project-specific execution risks (medium risk) 
64. 
Credit risk: GCF is exposed to the credit risk of the local financial institutions (LFIs) 
under the loan facility to be established (activity 3.3.2). GCF is the sole financier for the loan 
facility, therefore the AE does not have equal exposure to the LFIs during the project 
implementation. The local banks and micro finance institutions that benefit from the loan 
facility will be selected by the AE based on the criteria described in the funding proposal. The 
AE’s due diligence on the LFIs and the LFIs’ capacity to identify and finance pipeline projects 
will be important.  
65. 
Security conditions: the funding proposal identified deteriorating security conditions, 
including terrorist attack in the project area as one of the risks. The AE puts in a mitigation 
measure involving cooperating with regional and communal security services and set up an 
alert system in case of terrorist attack. In addition, to mitigate the risk of vandalism and/or theft 
of project equipment, the equipment will be managed with marked identification numbers. The 
project will require that the supplier of the equipment integrate a geo-referenced warning 
system so that it can be tracked using GPS trackers.  
66. 
Operations and maintenance risk: Underperformance of solar pumps and irrigation 
systems will reduce the impact of the projects as well as the financial viability of the project. 
ONAHA will be the owner of the assets (e.g. perimeters, solar pumps and solar panels). The 
farmers’ cooperatives will be in charge of the maintenance of assets but under the ONAHA’s 
control. The cooperative will collect water and energy bills from the users and pay ONAHA. The 
EE’s capacity to procure the quality service provider for drilling boreholes for irrigation systems 
and procuring high-quality irrigation and solar systems will be critical for the sustainability of 
the project. The funding proposal states that the national Solar Energy Centre (CNES) will 
control the quality and efficiency of solar equipment and pumps. In addition, the equipment 
must be certified by international experts experienced in the context of the project areas in 
Niger.  
67. 
Project viability and concessionality: The viability of the project will depend on the 
EE’s capacity to manage new and old hydro-agricultural areas with grant financing and scale up 
the climate smart agriculture activities in the form of loan facility. The economic and financial 
analysis results in an economic internal rate of return of 35.4 per cent considering the crop 
yields and saved energy and water cost as the main benefits, with operating cost and 
amortization of the loans over 25 years.  
68. 
For Activity 3.3.2 (loan facility), the pricing of the GCF loan will be 0.8% per annum 
(including service fee) and BOAD will on lend to the selected LFIs. In addition to the GCF 
interest rate, the LFIs will charge a credit risk premium based on the risk profile of the end 
beneficiaries. BOAD will monitor this closely to ensure that end borrowers benefit from GCF 
concessionality and that the interest rate charged by the LFIs shall be at least 300 bps lower 
than the prevailing market rate in Niger. As per the term sheet, the AE shall submit a 
methodology detailing the mechanisms to ensure that GCF concessionality is effectively 
transferred down to the end beneficiaries.  
4.3.4. 
Compliance risk (medium risk) 
69. 
The Niger is not subject to targeted United Nations Security Council (UNSC) resolutions.

=== PAGE 116 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add. 08 
Page 116 
 
 
70. 
The Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD, West African Development 
Bank), as the AE, confirms that no person or entity on a UNSC sanctions list, including the United 
Nations consolidated sanctions list, will be involved in any way in the project or its activities, 
whether as a counterpart, enforcer, or beneficiary. 
71. 
BOAD will utilize all available measures to confirm that no individual or entity that is 
listed on any UNSC sanctions list, including the United Nations consolidated sanctions list will 
be involved in any manner with the project or its activities, either as a counterparty, 
implementer, or beneficiary. The BOAD attaches importance to this verification. 
72. 
The BOAD financial security structure uses Dow Jones Risk Centre and Dow Jones Risk 
and Compliance Premium databases to access detailed information on politically exposed 
persons, close associates, state-owned companies, involvement in serious crimes, as well as 
more than 2000 sanctions lists (OFAC, EU, UN etc.) and related lists (FBI, Interpol, etc.). BOAD 
also reviews corporate entities for adverse media and ownership or association with sanctioned 
persons. 
73. 
BOAD advises that a preliminary assessment of money-laundering (ML) and terrorist 
financing (TF) risks has determined a “medium” probability of occurrence, with a “medium” 
impact to project activities. As a mitigation measure, BOAD undertakes to conduct an 
assessment and thorough due diligence on any beneficiaries and counterparties before 
commencing project financing activities. This is a standard practice and policy as part of BOAD’s 
project approval process. 
74. 
BOAD is committed to conducting anti-money laundering and countering the financing 
of terrorism (AML/CFT) due diligence as part of its prevention strategy against ML/TF risks. 
BOAD has developed a Financial Security Policy (2016), an internal document that constitutes 
the general framework of its control system, covering all BOAD activities relating to the 
prevention, surveillance and management of ML/TF risks. 
75. 
The Financial Security Policy requires recipients to disclose information about its 
clients’ transactions to relevant authorities in cases where international rules and local laws 
oblige regulated financial institutions to do so, including ML/TF cases. Furthermore, AML/CFT 
due diligence procedures established by the BOAD policy will take into account: 
(a) 
An assessment of the risks and vulnerabilities of ML/TF, prohibited practices and 
integrity issues in the project, activities and counterparties; 
(b) 
The level of detail undertaken for the said assessment, appraisal, evaluation, 
consideration; 
(c) 
A summary of any risks and vulnerabilities identified;  
(d) 
An indication of severity for those risks and vulnerabilities; and 
(e) 
Mitigation strategies proposed to avoid and control those risks and vulnerabilities. 
76. 
Under its due diligence process, BOAD will assess civil/criminal and regulatory 
antecedents and sanctions lists. BOAD will also conduct administrative investigations into 
corruption, fraud, coercion, collisions, and inconvenient practices, and refer matters to national 
authorities for criminal investigations where necessary. BOAD has integrated measures within 
the Bank Group’s internal operations to combat illicit financial flows and ML/TF.   
77. 
BOAD confirms that it will conduct a due diligence assessment when private financial 
institutions (i.e. banks, microfinance institutions, etc.) are selected. However, BOAD advises that 
this procedure does not apply to the Government of the Niger as it is a member State of BOAD.  
78. 
In the context of this project, BOAD confirms that it will ensure that the funds financing 
the project are not of illicit origin or related to fraud against Niger’s financial interests, 
corruption, organized criminal activities, terrorism or drug trafficking. BOAD undertakes to:

(a) 
Forward to GCF without delay any information raising suspicions as to unlawfulness of 
sums invested in the company and/or the project;

80.

81. 
However, BOAD has indicated that the financing tools used may consist of short-, 
medium- and long-term lending, cash advances for feasibility studies, equity investments, 
financing arrangements and/or bond loan guarantees. Risk assessments for such distributions 
will be conducted for all private institutions or people involved in the project to prevent fraud, 
abuse, ML/TF, or prohibited practices in the management of financial operation.   
82. 
As the project involves a community funding instrument, BOAD is particularly 
concerned about the proper allocation of loans and guarantees that it will grant. BOAD 
acknowledges that the project may be a target for criminals or malicious individuals to launder 
money in the context of loan repayments or the artificial stake of a guarantee. In order to 
comply with international and community obligations, BOAD has implemented a financial 
security system, which includes:  
(a) 
The Financial Security Policy, applicable to the prevention and fight against ML/TF that 
defines the general framework; and  
(b) 
The Procedures Manual for the Prevention and Control of Money Laundering and 
Terrorist Financing, which is the operational version of the BOAD’s Financial Security 
Policy.   
83. 
BOAD advises that analyses of procurement processes will include AML/CFT due 
diligence as part of its strategy to prevent and combat ML/TF risks. The Procedures Manual for 
the Prevention and Control of Money Laundering and Terrorist Financing constitutes the 
general framework of the control system covering all of BOAD's activities relating to the 
prevention, surveillance and management of ML/TF risks.  
84. 
BOAD has extensive lending experience in the region utilizing its current ML/TF risk 
management framework, with a lending portfolio to financial institutions of over USD 1.3 billion 
within the UEMOA region, and approximately USD 568 million for its sovereign lending 
portfolio in Niger alone. 
85. 
Additionally, ONAHA will provide supervision over the technical execution of the 
project. Annual audits will be undertaken and reported to the Ministry of Agriculture, the 
Ministry of Planning and BOAD, ensuring any materials or technology procured under this 
project are used only for the purposes intended and are not diverted or misused for

=== PAGE 118 ===

Page 118 
 
 
unauthorized, improper or illicit purposes. If there are any inconsistencies or use of funds that 
are not justified through validated accounting documents, no other disbursements will be made 
by BOAD.  
86. 
Under its due diligence process, BOAD will assess civil/criminal and regulatory 
antecedents and sanctions lists. BOAD also conducts administrative investigations into 
corruption, fraud, coercion, collisions, and inconvenient practices, and make use of the relevant 
national authorities for the necessary criminal investigations. 
87. 
BOAD has established a grievance mechanism through its Grievance Policy and 
Procedures Manual which is an independent mechanism through which individuals who have 
been harmed because of a project financed or implemented by BOAD can file a complaint. This 
manual defines the complaint resolution mechanism in the implementation of any project 
financed or implemented by BOAD. The grievance mechanism is coordinated and managed by 
the Compliance and Regulatory Division of BOAD.   
88. 
Recommended risk rating: The Office of Risk Management and Compliance

this request proceeding to the next steps at this time.
