Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP176 
Proposal name: 
Hydro-agricultural development with smart agriculture 
practices resilient to climate change in Niger 
Accredited entity: 
Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement (BOAD)   
Country/(ies): 
Niger 
Project/programme size: 
Medium 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
 
 
        Scale: High 
1. 
This funding proposal for a Medium-sized Cross-cutting project, in environmental and 
social safeguards Category B, is submitted by the Republic of the Niger (otherwise known as 
Niger), an African State with least developed country (LDC) status. The total cost of the funding 
proposal is EUR 45.54 million, including a EUR 30.14 million Green Climate Fund (GCF) grant 
contribution, which forms 66 per cent of the total cost. The remaining EUR 15.4 million, or 34 
per cent of the total project cost, is made up of grant contributions from the Government of 
Niger (EUR 6.16 million), and concessional loan finance from BOAD – the Banque Ouest 
Africaine de Développement, the Accredited Entity (EUR 9.24 million).  
2. 
The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) assesses the impact potential of this 
cross-cutting project as HIGH, including both mitigation and adaptation aspects. In the past 
three decades, climate change has increased the frequency and intensity of drought and flooding 
in Niger, with the five project regions of Tahoua, Agadez, Maradi, Diffa and Zinder particularly 
vulnerable to these growing hazards. Many of these regions’ Irrigated Agricultural 
Developments (Aménagements Hydro-Agricoles or “AHAs”), vital to food security through 
irrigated staple crops rice, millet, maize and cowpea, are being increasingly damaged by 
siltation from flooding. Increased temperatures, combined with an overall declining trend in 
total annual rainfall and a more erratic pattern, are increasing evapotranspiration and causing 
drought periods, which means it is increasingly important to expand the area under irrigation. 
The low-emissions, climate-resilient development pathway for this expansion involves using 
boreholes for sustainable extraction of groundwater and flood-proofed improved irrigation 
systems for water and energy efficiency, powered by renewable energy technology.  
3. 
The project aims to contribute to the increase of national agricultural production by 
strengthening the resilience of populations to the adverse effects of climate change, through 
three components of work:  
(a) 
Component 1 involves the expansion and climate-proofing of the AHAs against siltation 
(caused by flooding intensified by climate change) in sites with untapped groundwater. 
This is extracted on a sustainable basis using water-efficient drip irrigation schemes 
with solar-PV powered pumps (instead of high-emissions diesel-powered pumps). An 
area of 749 hectares of AHAs will be rehabilitated, and 1,000 new hectares of irrigated 
plots will be developed. Flood control mechanisms will be put in place, constructing 
embankments and channels for drainage and aquifer recharge, and structures and 
revegetation against erosion.

=== PAGE 122 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 122 
 
 
 
 
(b) 
Component 2 involves maximizing the productivity of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) in 
the irrigated areas of the AHAs, through land governance in which plots are allocated, 
beneficiaries’ rights are registered and cooperatives are strengthened. Improved 
climate-resilient farming will be promoted, using agro-climatological information 
services to farmers, and supply of inputs including drought-resilient seeds. Operation 
and maintenance and monitoring systems will be set up for irrigation and pumping 
equipment. Technical assistance will be provided for crop preservation, value addition, 
warehousing and market access, strengthening value chains for the produce of the 
AHAs.  
(c) 
Component 3 involves developing capacity to sustain CSA and irrigation, including 
mainstreaming climate risk management with officials and producers; developing 
capacity of local financial institutions; and lessons learning and knowledge sharing for 
scale-up outside the project regions. Output 3.3, managed directly by BOAD as Executing 
Entity (EE), will enhance access to finance for climate-smart farming and value chain 
development, through establishing an Agricultural Loan Facility at BOAD. The facility, 
the details of which still need to be worked out, will on-lend to carefully selected local 
financial institutions who in turn will make affordable loans to producer and processing 
cooperatives. 
4. 
Mitigation impact: The cross-cutting project is expected to have a modest but 
significant impact through GHG emissions avoided as a result of the 23 newly established and 
expanded AHAs being based on appropriately sized solar photo-voltaic (PV) power systems, in 
contrast to a without-project scenario in which irrigation schemes would have continued to 
expand using over-capacitated and high-emissions diesel generators for pumping water. It is 
anticipated that the project interventions will achieve both better energy efficiency and a clean 
energy path through switching of fuels. This will result in total avoided emissions of 227,551 
tons of CO2-equivalent, i.e., 11,176.3 tCO2-equ. per year over the 25-year lifetime of the project. 
The basis for using CDM methodology ASM-I-F and the calculations are set out in full in Annex 
22, together with monitoring and reporting arrangements, which involve equipping 10% of the 
PV systems with energy meters. Responsibility for monitoring of the energy generation and 
avoided emissions is taken by the NDA, the National Commission for Environment and 
Sustainable Development (CNEDD), with collection of data by the EE, ONAHA (the National 
Office of Hydro Agricultural Development), through its Director of Infrastructure of Materials 
and Workrooms. 
5. 
The common practice in Niger of using diesel-generated pumps to extract water from 
boreholes results in high emissions of CO2, NO2 and particulate matter, and causes air pollution. 
The independent TAP welcomes the shift through the project’s investment to a clean and 
renewable energy pathway, given the importance for the country of maximizing sustainable 
groundwater extraction, in line with the Ministry of Agriculture’s Strategy for small-scale 
irrigation systems (SPIN). This investment, whilst costly, avoids the highly undesirable 
alternative of locking in a new generation of high-emission infrastructure, since diesel pumps 
have a lifetime of 20-25 years. The investment simultaneously increases the number of farming 
households with access to low-emission energy for purposes such as mobile phone charging, 
charging lighting systems and other portable battery-based appliances. The project also intends 
to help to remove barriers to scale-up of this low-emission energy, through provision of access 
to finance, though this is not without its challenges (see Paradigm shift section). 
6. 
The independent TAP notes that, whilst this shift from fossil fuel-based to renewable 
energy source for powering borehole pumps is wholly positive in terms of mitigation impact, 
such a shift can pose the risk of potential maladaptation. Once the upfront capital is invested in 
the solar PV systems, the constraint on pumping that was previously posed by the cost of the 
diesel fuel is removed, and if this is combined with inadequate water governance mechanisms, 
there is potential for overextraction of groundwater – see section below on “Risks of

=== PAGE 123 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 123 
 
 
 
 
maladaptation”, for clarification on how this risk has been adequately addressed by the 
proponents in the design of this project. 
7. 
Adaptation impact: The project will help agricultural communities in the five most 
vulnerable regions of Niger to adapt to the negative effects of climate change – reducing their 
vulnerability to flood and drought through investment in efficient, resilient irrigation systems,  
and enhancing their adaptive capacity through support to climate-smart agriculture (CSA), and 
strengthening of value chains for CSA produce. Meteorological trends recorded in recent 
decades in these five regions of Niger on key climate parameters are aligned with future 
projections, and include increasing average temperatures, later onset of the rainy season, and 
more dry spells during the rainy season – all of which combine to cause increased 
evapotranspiration and loss of soil moisture, placing stress on staple crop yields, especially 
where irrigation is unavailable or inadequate. In addition, the project helps these communities 
adapt to a growing frequency of intense rainfall events – as shown by trends recorded in recent 
decades, aligned with future projections – which cause flash floods and soil erosion that 
combine to cause silting up of irrigation and drainage channels (rendering 20% of the AHA 
system unusable in the five project regions after the last major floods).  
8. 
The project will rehabilitate existing damaged AHA plots and irrigation schemes and 
install new ones, using the efficient California and drip irrigation systems in place of the old 
open-channel irrigation systems which had high evaporation losses (worsening with climate 
change). These interventions will both increase water availability for irrigation (drought 
resilience) and ensure that the new schemes are protected against damage and siltation (flood 
resilience). The newly irrigated areas will be able to have two, and in most cases three, 
agricultural growing seasons per year, since they will no longer be reliant on rainfall. In addition 
to enhancing water supply through the new boreholes, the improved efficiency of the new 
irrigation systems will mean that water loss through evaporation is significantly reduced, 
allowing an increase in actual water availability for rice cultivation, for example, from 60% to 
85% – critical in the face of declining overall rainfall and seasonal shortages. The independent 
TAP also welcomes the integrated approach taken to drought and flood management – as seen 
in the construction of wide drainage channels to divert excess water during flash floods away 
from the AHA plots, and direct them into the downstream seasonal rivers or “koris”, from where 
the water can seep into the ground, recharging the aquifer.  
9. 
The number of expected direct beneficiaries of the project is 121,615 people, made up as 
follows: (i) 56,056 people from farming households in the new AHAs; (ii) 41,895 people in the 
AHAs to be rehabilitated; (iii) 12,320 beneficiaries of the line of credit for private financial 
sector for scaling up; (iv) 10,920 beneficiaries of income-generating activities (39 groups with 
an average of 40 participating households each); and (v) 424 officials who will benefit from 
capacity-building activities. The number of indirect beneficiaries is estimated at 1 million, based 
on the total population in the villages, communes and regions benefiting from the project, who 
will purchase and/or consume the expanded produce of the AHAs; participate in awareness-
raising activities; and receive agro-meteorological information, inputs on good practices, and 
radio programming. Through Component 2, the project will also lead to strengthening of 
institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and development. The 
focus on use of agro-climatological information in crop planning will lead to an increase in 
generation and use of climate information in decision-making. 
10. 
Risk of maladaptation: A significant risk in groundwater-based projects in general is 
that the introduction of solar PV technology for pumping (removing the former constraint of the 
cost of diesel), combined with inadequate water governance mechanisms, can allow 
overextraction leading to a groundwater deficit – when water is pumped out faster than the rate 
at which the aquifer is naturally replenished. This lowers the water table, eventually rendering 
the wellpoint unusable, or necessitating deeper “mining” of water that risks contamination by 
nitrates. As the water table drops, other wells in the area dry up, as do springs and ponds, with

=== PAGE 124 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 124 
 
 
 
 
harmful effects on biodiversity and human health. In many regions of the world, multiple poorly 
sited wellpoints have also caused land subsidence, where the soil around the depleted 
aquifer collapses, compacts and drops, damaging assets and livelihoods.  
11. 
Following questions posed to the Accredited Entity (AE) on why this risk was not 
highlighted and how it is being managed, the independent TAP was reassured, both on the 
process followed for selecting sites and determining extraction rates, and on the strong water 
governance mechanisms in place. The AE highlighted the site selection process outlined in the 
feasibility study, with thresholds used to ensure that only areas where remote sensing has 
indicated significant untapped potential were selected. The selection was based on full 
groundwater analyses for the Iullemeden and Chad Basin Aquifer Systems, allowing for 
cumulative impacts of other schemes, with the “Water estimation and water balance analysis” 
figures shown in Annex 28.  The independent TAP notes the conclusion in the Feasibility Study 
that “the project will exert an acceptable level of pressure that is within the aquifer’s recharge 
capacities, even allowing for an ecological flow reserve”.  
12. 
In response to questions from the independent TAP, the AE also highlighted that 
pumping will be allowed for no more than 5 hours per day, and that this will be controlled by 
relay timers in the pumping systems. The new boreholes will all be fitted with piezometers, to 
enable proactive and ongoing monitoring of groundwater pressure, to feed into wider 
monitoring of the impact of extraction on the groundwater resource. The project will also 
establish or strengthen and capacitate Irrigation Water Users Associations (AUEIs), which will 
be responsible for managing the pumps, and will sign contracts with the EE ONAHA that specify 
the constraints on pumping, and enforce sanctions if these are exceeded. The AE provided 
additional detail on the water governance mechanism, outlining the specific roles and 
responsibilities of each Irrigation Water User Association and of ONAHA, as set out in the 
operating contracts between the parties.  
1.2 
Paradigm shift potential                                                            Scale: Medium 
13. 
The paradigm shift involved in the proposed project is rated as MEDIUM, with potential 
to increase once the loan component is fully designed and applied in the market, should this be 
feasible, enabling scaling up through crowding in the private sector.  
14. 
The project is well designed to support new approaches in irrigation and agriculture, 
incorporating climate risk management principles in an integrated approach to flood and 
drought hazards that helps agrarian communities adapt. The project contributes towards the 
creation of new business activities and local markets for the produce of the new efficient and 
climate-resilient AHAs. It makes a small but clear contribution towards putting the country on a 
low-carbon development pathway in support of urgent global targets – enabling the crucial shift 
away from high-emissions diesel pumps to solar PV. The project also has a good orientation 
towards knowledge management and sharing learning at local scale, strengthening producers’ 
and officials’ technical and organizational capacities, and their understanding of climate change 
and resilient farming methods. The collaboration on the dissemination of agri-climatological 
information through mobile phones and radio broadcasts between the meteorological services, 
the National Solar Energy Centre (CNES), AGRHYMET (Centre Régional d'Agriculture, 
d'Hydrologie et de Metéorologie) and the Development Directorate, indicates potential for 
national upscaling in this area, though no specific pathways are provided in the project design. 
15. 
The extent to which the project is able to bring about a real paradigm shift will depend 
on whether the project interventions can be replicated post-project in other villages in the 
target regions, and scaled up nationally. The groundwater analyses summarized in the 
feasibility study indicate that Niger has large and wide-scale untapped groundwater potential, 
whose utilization will be at the heart of the country’s adaptation strategy (as set out in the 
PANA). In the absence of massive additional development aid to the Government, beyond the

=== PAGE 125 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 125 
 
 
 
 
current set of parallel initiatives, the cost of realizing full-scale sustainable utilization of 
groundwater resources will need to be borne, at least in part, by loans from local financial 
institutions to producer associations, made possible as the costs of renewable energy 
technologies continue to come down. 
16. 
Scaling up climate-resilient irrigated agriculture through crowding in the private sector 
in the form of local commercial finance institutions (as well as companies involved in agri-
processing value chains) depends on the successful execution and creation of long-lasting 
impact of the proposed loan finance element of the project. Through the EUR 6 million 
Agricultural Loan Facility, GCF funds will be on-lent by BOAD, following a rigorous due diligence 
process, to the national agricultural bank BAGRI and commercial local financial institutions 
(LFIs), who will make loans with reasonable interest rates and on more favourable terms than 
are currently available in the market to aggregated producer groups who can provide a 
collective collateral. The component may also include a guarantee fund. At present, however, the 
loan finance element of the project is not described in detail in the Funding Proposal and 
annexes, meaning that fundamental information on the market potential, in particular uptake 
and scalability, is missing, raising questions about whether the component would be able to 
drive lasting market change.  
17. 
In response to questions on this from the independent TAP, the AE indicated that the 
loan element of the project was added at a late stage, following interactions with the GCF, and 
was therefore not as fully developed as the other parts of the project. Nevertheless, the AE 
considered that there was potential for the loan component, and that its basic design, to work 
through co-operatives, would enable it to reach farmers normally not considered creditworthy. 
One bank had already indicated interest. 
18. 
The independent TAP notes that the loan element of the project has not been as 
thoroughly designed as the grant element, which had a full feasibility study including detailed 
groundwork on climate change impacts, groundwater studies, site selection, water balances, 
land governance issues, and crop yield projections. By contrast, the independent TAP has not 
seen detail on consultations with the financial sector, or on the design of the on-lending 
mechanism and guarantee fund. A number of areas remain unclear, for example: 
(a) 
the anticipated role of additional private sector investment, including the specifics of 
blending of the LFIs’ funds into loans with the GCF money; 
(b) 
the likely size of the market with cooperative borrowers for specific financial products 
and the terms which would be appropriate;  
(c) 
the anticipated default rates and how these are taken into account in a financial model 
analysing potential loan performance;  
(d) 
the exit strategy for the loan facility and how future loans without the GCF subsidy 
would be effectively derisked; and 
(e) 
how linkages will be established with the closely related GCF SAP 012, approved at B.24. 
19. 
 The independent TAP furthermore remains concerned about the capacity of the 
targeted borrower associations to undertake comprehensive financial management, and thus 
their ability to repay loans, whilst also covering the costs of operations and maintenance post-
project. This could undermine, rather than strengthen private sector involvement, if the failure 
rate were to be very high, in turn leading to higher risk being associated with the sector as a 
whole.  
20. 
Notwithstanding this risk, enhancing the bankability and financial management capacity 
of village savings and loans schemes, as well as of water user and producer associations would 
seem like a logical first step to take prior encouraging them to take on the risk of commercial 
debt. The FP correctly identifies the many barriers to drawing in this largely unbanked sector, 
but fails to analyze in detail and provide clear steps how these barriers can be overcome. In this

=== PAGE 126 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 126 
 
 
 
 
regard, the independent TAP considers the development of a detailed and strong technical 
assistance programme for both banks and borrowers to be indispensable. The independent TAP 
is thus recommending the undertaking of an in-depth market and design study that ensures that 
the Agricultural Loan Facility is viable, will be of unequivocal benefit to vulnerable farmers, and 
will be effectively set up to crowd in future investment. This should include, if the potential for 
market uptake is found to be positive, a detailed technical assistance programme. 
1.3 
Sustainable development potential                       Scale: Medium to high 
21. 
The project’s sustainable development potential overall is rated as MEDIUM TO HIGH. It 
is not rated as High because of concerns about the enormous barriers that exist to achieving full 
participation by women in economic opportunities and decision-making related to the project. 
The FP is accompanied by a solid Gender Analysis and Action Plan, but there seems to be a gap 
between the socio-economic constraints women face and the ambitious targets in the plan, that 
is not fully addressed by innovative thinking in the design of the project activities.  
1.3.1. 
Environmental co-benefits 
22. 
The project’s actions to combat erosion, flooding and silting up of the irrigated plots will 
include planting of local tree and other plant species, and the practice of Farmer Managed 
Natural  Regeneration and agroforestry will be beneficial to the restoration and maintenance of 
biodiversity, including plants, birds, small mammals, insects and micro-organisms. The climate-
smart agriculture methods that will be promoted as part of the project will improve soil quality 
and productivity, and facilitate carbon storage in the soil. 
1.3.2. 
Social co-benefits  
23. 
The project has a number of important social co-benefits, including: strengthening 
cooperative associations for irrigation, farming and agri-processing; providing economic 
opportunities for unemployed youth and thus reducing the phenomenon of rural exodus; 
improving the food security of beneficiaries through increasing yields by between 12 and 87%, 
and enabling two to three agricultural campaigns per year, instead of one, on the new AHA 
plots; enhancing communities’ access to agricultural extension, particularly for women not 
previously exposed to such opportunities, and including female headed households; and 
ensuring continuous, affordable, clean and safe energy access for agriculture. 
1.3.3. 
Economic co-benefits 
24. 
The implementation of the project will create direct jobs in the construction of the plots, 
sinking of boreholes and construction of flood defences around the perimeters. These jobs will 
mainly involve local labour and there will also be new indirect jobs around the sites for 
operations and maintenance, and supply and distribution channels for raw and semi-finished 
agricultural products. Approximately 12,000 farmers will benefit, not counting the labour for 
clearing, ploughing, sowing, maintenance, harvesting, etc. The project will enable the 
development of agricultural marketing chains as well as product processing and conservation 
activities. Enhanced yields and new income streams will be combined with savings on diesel 
costs to free up cash for operations and maintenance, and, for some cooperatives with capacity, 
to take loans to further expand production and processing facilities. 
1.3.4. 
Gender-sensitivity and social inclusion  
25. 
The FP acknowledges the highly constrained socio-economic position of women in rural 
Niger, and the high rate of youth under- and unemployment, and states that “In the selection of

=== PAGE 127 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 127 
 
 
 
 
direct beneficiaries, the gender criterion is included to give women, young people and adults the 
opportunity to participate fully in the project. Jobs will be created for women in sowing, 
maintenance, harvesting, threshing, marketing, etc. as well as for men. About 50% of direct and 
indirect beneficiaries will be women”. A thorough Gender Analysis and Action Plan outlines 
numerical targets to ensure women’s participation in decision-making structures, and their 
access to technical assistance and finance, but there seems to be a gap between the socio-
economic realities and the ambitious targets in the plan, that is not fully addressed by the design 
of the project activities. For example, the Analysis notes that “Women's fertility rate is one of the 
highest in Africa, 7.6 children per woman and can reach 9 in regions such as Maradi and Zinder” 
but no mention is made of mechanisms to support women’s participation, such as cooperative 
childcare facilities as part of the project interventions. 
26. 
On the challenge that “It is common in rural areas for women to speak little and/or to 
agree with the views and decisions of men”, the plan suggests a rather vague solution: “To allow 
or even encourage women to speak up and express their divergent or concordant points of view 
during the various meetings related to the project.” In response to a question from the 
independent TAP about the experience with FAO Dimitra clubs as a vehicle for social 
empowerment and women’s leadership in the project region, the AE responded that “Since 
2009, 550 Dimitra listening clubs have been set up in Niger, in 129 villages in the Tillabéry and 
Dosso regions.  Among the Five Regions covered by the project (Agadez, Diffa, Maradi, Tahoua, 
Zinder), it is reported that listening clubs have been set up in the Maradi region since 2013.  The 
Project can draw inspiration from it as an organizing mechanism in Output 3.2, especially for 
engaging women and youth.” The independent TAP notes the value of separate forums where 
women are able to build up confidence and skills to participate in mixed activities and 
organizational structures that happen in parallel.1  
27. 
 In response to a request from independent TAP to elaborate on how it will be ensured 
that “the poorest and most-climate vulnerable smallholder landowners” will have access to AHA 
plots, the AE highlighted the secure land framework whereby all the land to be developed will 
be registered in the name of ONAHA, which will sign an operating contract with each beneficiary 
(man, woman, landowner or not) giving rise to the payment of an annual royalty. Each 0.125 ha 
plot will be allocated to a farmer (male, female, young) or to a household and its members 
(headed by a man or a woman). Women living in polygamous unions may, if they wish, be 
allocated plots under their own names, where the woman and her children will be consider as 
household. The AHAs system also includes clear mechanisms for land tenure conflict 
management and dispute resolution.  
1.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
               
                    Scale: High 
28. 
Because of the demonstrably high vulnerability of the country to a range of external 
shocks and stresses, and the high exposure of beneficiary groups to climate risks, as well as the 
overall income and development level of Niger, the Needs of the recipient are rated as HIGH. 
Niger is a Landlocked Least Developed Country (LDC) and is among the Low Income and Deficit 
Countries (PFRDV). Niger is the tenth poorest country in the world in terms of GDP per capital, 
and is ranked last in terms of the Human Development Index, with its economy highly 
dependent on agriculture. Before the COVID pandemic, a quarter of the population was 
classified as vulnerable to multidimensional poverty, and this is likely to have increased in the 
last two years. 
 
1 See , for example, Olumide Adisa (2020) Rural women’s participation in solar-powered irrigation in Niger: lessons 
from Dimitra Clubs, Gender & Development, 28:3, 535-549, DOI: 10.1080/13552074.2020.1833483

=== PAGE 128 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 128 
 
 
 
 
29. 
Niger is also one of the hottest and driest countries in the world, with most of its area 
covered by the southward-encroaching Sahara Desert, and even the Sahel region of the country 
is subject to serious climate change threats. For rural populations for whom agriculture is the 
main source of income and livelihoods, the negative effects of climate change are felt most 
severely. Pressure on natural resources such as water and grazing is also one of the underlying 
causes of farmer-herder conflicts and violent extremism in the region. From 1983 to 2013, the 
average temperature increased by 0.35oC per decade. Rainfall has been generally declining with 
isohyet sliding southwards. There has been a sequence of agricultural droughts since the 1980s, 
with domestic agricultural production currently structurally in deficit. 
30. 
Despite the ongoing efforts of the Government of Niger and its financial and technical 
partners to strengthen the resilience of the agriculture and water sectors and enhance climate 
resilience and food security, greater investment is needed. Niger’s fiscal gap is a barrier to 
addressing financing needs. Niger has a per-capita gross domestic product (GDP) of USD 565. It 
had a significant balance of payments deficit on its current account of over USD 1.8 billion in 
2020, and has a B3 credit rating by Moody’s. This suggests the country is just able to service 
foreign debt, and will be in a position to manage the concessional loan being provided by BOAD 
as co-finance to the project, but relies also on grant finance for the bulk of the project. 
1.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
                    Scale: High 
31. 
Country ownership of the proposed project is rated as HIGH, since it demonstrates 
strong alignment with national policies and strategies, and a high degree of engagement under 
the leadership of the NDA, CNEDD, by the EE, the Ministry of Agriculture, and its ONAHA agency, 
as well as a range of other public sector actors, and a growing engagement with the private 
sector. It is noted that the Government selected the Banque Ouest Africaine de Développement 
(BOAD) as the Accredited Entity for the project as a whole, and also as the Executing Entity for 
the GCF-funded agricultural loan facility under Output 3.3, building on the long established 
partnership between the Government of Niger and BOAD. 
32. 
 The project is well aligned with all of Niger’s key policies, strategies, plans and 
programmes, including its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) in terms of the Paris 
Agreement; the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PANA); the Sustainable Development 
Strategy and Inclusive Growth Vision 2035; the 3N Initiative for Food and Nutrition Security 
and Sustainable Agricultural Development ('Nigeriens feed Nigeriens'); the Small Irrigation 
Strategy in Niger; and the National Environmental Protection Policy. In particular, the project 
addresses several PANA priorities, viz. the adaptation of the agriculture and water sectors 
(diversification and intensification of irrigated crops; mobilization of surface water and 
groundwater exploitation; promotion of income-generating activities and the development of 
mutual aid associations). 
33. 
The independent TAP notes that the project will contribute directly to the 
implementation of the 2017-2020 Four-Year Contract-Plan between the National Office of 
Hydro-Agricultural Development (ONAHA) and the Niger State, promoting the goals that: (a) the 
productive base (AHA) is permanently functional; (b) irrigated agriculture on AHAs is 
sustainably productive and profitable; (c) the day-to-day management of AHAs is rational and 
efficient; and (d) investments in new AHA or rehabilitations are made in such a way that they 
are functional and sustainable, as well as climate-resilient. This four-year contract-plan targets 
4,000 hectares of new hydro-agricultural development (of which the project contributes 1,000), 
and 2,000 ha rehabilitated AHAs (of which the project contributes 749) and the construction of 
8 water mobilization works. The independent TAP considers that this direct alignment with 
public sector delivery targets is positive and will ensure a high degree of engagement 
throughout the project’s implementation.

=== PAGE 129 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 129 
 
 
 
 
34. 
The project was developed following a thorough stakeholder engagement process on the 
public side, involving the NDA, Agriculture Ministry (the Directorates of Agriculture, Livestock, 
Land tenure, Investment, Irrigation, ONAHA), the National Environmental Assessment Office, 
the Ministry of Environment (Directorates of Hydrology, ESIA), the Ministries of Planning, 
Energy and Finance, including the Directorate in charge of Microfinance Institutions. The 
National Agriculture Bank (BAGRI) was consulted and has expressed interest in cooperating on 
the Agricultural Loan Facility, but full engagement of local financial institutions is still to take 
place, reflecting the late inclusion of this element in the project design.  
35. 
At the local scale, consultations took place in 25 villages, 22 communes and 15 
departments across the regions of Maradi, Tahoua, Agadez, Zinder and Diffa. Regional 
Governors, Representatives of the Departments, Representatives of Municipalities, agriculture 
cooperatives and groups, breeders’ groups, landowners in all regions, Regional, departmental 
and communal offices of Agriculture, Livestock, Land tenure, Investment, Irrigation, ONAHA, 
Environment, Hydrology, Environmental and Social Impact Assessment and Energy were all 
consulted. Civil society and farmers' organizations (mixed and women's) actively contributed to 
the preparation of the project through the public consultations organized in the beneficiary 
villages. During these consultations, vulnerable groups expressed their interest in and support 
for the project activities. 
1.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
          Scale: Medium to high 
36. 
The project’s overall efficiency and effectiveness is rated at MEDIUM TO HIGH. The total 
project cost is EUR 45.54 million, including a EUR 30.14 million GCF grant contribution, which 
forms 66 per cent of the total cost. The remaining EUR 15.4 million, or 34 per cent of the total 
project cost, is made up of grant contributions from the Government of Niger (EUR 6.16 
million), and Senior Loan finance from the AE BOAD (EUR 9.24 million) which will support 
implementation of all the project components. The in-kind contributions from the Government 
of Niger and its commitment to waive taxes and customs duties on items procured for the 
project can be considered significant, given that Niger is an LDC with a limited revenue base.  
37. 
An important contribution during the project implementation period could come from 
of blended finance from Niger’s Local Financial Institutions. The national agriculture bank, 
BAGRI, has already expressed written interest in participating in the Agricultural Loan Facility. 
Through the facility, GCF funds will be on-lent by BOAD to eligible local financial institutions, 
who will blend the GCF funds with their own funds to make loans to farming and agri-
processing cooperatives. This will enable successful producer associations and those involved in 
preservation, warehousing and processing to expand their operations. It could also provide 
finance for the upfront capital investment by cooperatives who do not yet have irrigation, to 
access this technology. Such loans will build on the productivity gains stimulated through the 
project, and help to enable the long-term profitability and sustainability of irrigated climate-
smart agriculture and produce value chains. The details of the loan facility have not been 
convincingly worked out as yet, leaving an element of uncertainty on this. 
38. 
The Government of Niger wishes to carry out climate-resilient hydro-agricultural 
developments, with financing of this project requested through a GCF grant and a limited loan 
facility to be managed by the EE, to compensate for the lack of financial capacity of the State and 
to avoid increasing the level of indebtedness. This GCF investment will have several benefits for 
Niger in terms of (i) adaptation of the most vulnerable populations; (ii) mitigation efforts, 
improvement of soil quality and conservation, (iii) promotion of irrigation based on clean 
energy; and (iv) rational management of irrigation water. The independent TAP is pleased to 
see the strong emphasis in the project on establishing systems for operation and maintenance 
(O&M) of the AHAs (including plots, flood defences, irrigation and electrical equipment), 
through the Irrigation Water Users Associations in partnership with ONAHA. This includes

=== PAGE 130 ===
GCF/B.30/02/Add.08 
Page 130 
 
 
 
 
setting up bank accounts with LFIs to receive water fees charged, and ongoing budgeting and 
planning to ensure that these are used to cover the costs of O&M beyond the project lifetime, 
thus reinforcing the project’s cost-effectiveness and long-term sustainability.  
39. 
The effectiveness of interventions will be ensured using best practice climate-smart 
agriculture practices, with technical assistance informed by ongoing collaboration with CGIAR 
(the Advisory Group for International Agricultural Research), including the CGIAR Research 
Program on Grain Legumes and Dryland Cereals, and the AGRHYMET Regional Centre. 
Implementation of these smart agriculture actions requires relatively little investment, and is 
cost-effective. The solar technology promoted, while costly to install, has a lifespan of 25-30 
years and its maintenance is relatively inexpensive. The profitability analysis of the developed 
irrigation areas (see Feasibility Study, Economic and Financial Analysis) shows that producers, 
in the short term, will be able to bear the costs of replacing irrigation systems with a lifespan of 
five to 10 years. Output 2.3 of the project ensures the establishment of an integrated system for 
operations and maintenance with costs covered by water user fees, enabling sustainability post-
project. 
40. 
The economic analysis conducted for the project, which aims to enable the farmers in 
the AHAs to carry out three agriculture campaigns per year, uses a conservative scenario of two 
campaigns per year, and even under this scenario, the project is feasible. The project Internal 
Rate of Return (IRR) is 35.48%, and its Profitability Index (PI) is 2.13, with a net present value 
of  EUR 51,411, which is positive. With the three-campaign scenario, the project economic rate 
of return (ERR) rises to 45.06%. The project lower ERR sensitivity analysis with the project 
pessimistic scenarios is 27.43%, as outlined in detail in Annex 3. 
41. 
The cost of the GCF investment is a modest EUR 30 per beneficiary, taking into account 
the 1 million villagers whose food security will be enhanced through the project. The project’s 
cost-effectiveness comes also from its cross-cutting nature – with, on the one hand, water 
efficiency and climate-proofing of irrigation investments contributing to climate change 
adaptation, and on the other hand, energy efficiency and fuel switching contributing to climate 
change mitigation. It is noted that 76% of the project’s benefits, corresponding to EUR 
22,905,467 of GCF funds invested, are considered to be contributing to adaptation, with 24% of 
GCF funds, or EUR 7,233,305, to mitigation. When just the mitigation portion of the GCF 
investment is taken into account, the estimated GCF cost per tCO2eq removed, is EUR 32. This is 
a high cost, and reflects the current reality of the high upfront cost of installing solar PV 
pumping systems – which means that these systems are still not price-competitive with the 
fossil-fuel based alternatives that currently dominate African markets. At the same time, the 
independent TAP sees this investment as positive, given the urgency of expanding sustainable 
groundwater utilization in the region to adapt to climate change, since it will avoid locking in a 
new generation of dirty diesel generators with high emissions and a lifetime of 20-25 years, 
which would be in contradiction with Niger’s NDC commitments. 
II. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
42. 
This is an excellent project which meets all of the GCF’s investment criteria. In order to 
ensure that it fulfils its potential in terms of achieving a paradigm shift through crowding in 
private finance, the independent TAP highlights the need for further development of the 
Agricultural Loan Facility. An in-depth market and design study is needed, to ensure that the 
facility and any associated guarantee funds are based on a feasible financial model, including 
consideration of likely default rates. Technical assistance to both lenders and borrowers must 
be well thought through to facilitate a transformational shift within the local banking sector, 
effectively derisking these kinds of loans for the future. The independent TAP therefore 
recommends this funding proposal for approval, subject to the following condition: 
Condition precedent to disbursement by GCF of funds for Output 3.3 under the FAA:

Gender Assessment

=== PAGE 136 ===
4 
 
Introduction  
This assessment aims to provide information and data on the gender situation in the design and 
implementation of the "Hydro-Agricultural Development Project with Climate Resilient Smart 
Agriculture Practices" in Niger. 
The project is structured around 3 components: i) Sustainable development and rehabilitation of 
perimeters vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change, ii) Support for the sustainable increase 
of agricultural productivity and farmers' income in irrigated areas on developed and rehabilitated 
perimeters, and iii) Support for the sustainable development of developed and rehabilitated perimeters 
The gender assessment analyzes gender inequalities in several areas and identifies opportunities that 
could be seized through project activities to close the gaps that exist within beneficiary communities. 
The information and data collected for this analysis is limited to the national and regional level. It will 
be supplemented with commune- and site-specific information and data prior to project 
implementation. They will serve as a basis for the development and implementation of a Gender 
Action Plan that aims more specifically to: 
o Improve the participation of women, youth and disadvantaged groups in decision-making and 
project implementation at all levels; 
o Improve the accessibility of women, youth and disadvantaged groups to the learning and 
training opportunities offered by the project; 
o Promote information and communication to integrate gender throughout the process; 
o Promote gender awareness among project team members and other local actors. 
1. Gender mainstreaming in Niger national institutional framework 
1.1. 
National initiatives 
In Niger, at the national level, the Ministry for the Promotion of Women and Child Protection (MPFPE) 
is the body responsible for coordinating actions for the promotion of women and gender equality. It 
includes a Directorate for the Promotion of Women and Gender which ensures, from an operational 
point of view, the coordination, monitoring and evaluation of interventions in the areas of the promotion 
of women and gender. The Ministry also has decentralized services that ensure the coordination, 
monitoring and evaluation of actions at the local level.  
At regional level, there are decentralized communities and municipalities which are directly involved in 
the implementation of PNG through their Development Plans. So, they work in close collaboration with 
the services of the Ministry in charge of Gender in order to ensure that gender is taken into account in 
all interventions. 
In 2018, the Government of Niger has adopted a National Gender Policy (PNG), the overall objective 
of which is to contribute to the creation of an able environment to the achievement of equity and 
equality of opportunity and opportunity. This PNG has four strategic axes: 
o 
Improvement of the socio-cultural environment in relation to demography, peace and security 
for more equity between men and women; 
o 
Strengthening of the institutional and legal framework favorable to the effective application of 
the rights of women and girls, to the fight against gender-based violence and to the equitable 
participation of men and women in the management of power; 
o 
Economic empowerment and inclusive growth in relation to sustainable management of the 
environment, disaster risk management, migration and humanitarian emergencies; 
o 
Strengthening of institutional mechanisms and organizational frameworks for coordination, 
monitoring-evaluation and partnership.

=== PAGE 137 ===
5 
 
In addition to this ministry, there are other institutional mechanisms contributing to the achievement of 
the same objective, notably: i) the Directorate for the Enrolment of Girls, which is the body responsible 
for implementing the government's policy on the enrolment of girls; ii) the Gender Advisors to the 
President of the Republic, the Prime Minister and the Ombudsman, who are responsible for providing 
technical advice on the inclusion of gender in the country's development policies and programs; iii) the 
National Observatory for the Promotion of Gender Equality (ONPG), which is responsible for the 
promotion of gender equality in the country's development policies and programs, (iii) the National 
Observatory for Gender Promotion (ONPG), created in 2015 and attached to the Prime Minister's 
office in 2017, is the monitoring mechanism for the promotion of gender equity and equality. The 
Observatory encourages institutions to pay particular attention to gender statistics and to integrate 
them into reports and assessments, iv) gender cells and focal points within sectoral ministries. The 
gender units were set up by ministerial order within the sectoral ministries under the aegis of the 
Ministry for the Advancement of Women and Child Protection. The role of the cells is to monitor the 
systematic inclusion of gender in the development policies, programs and projects of their ministry. 
Other institutions such as the Network of Nigerien Parliamentarians on Gender Issues, the National 
Commission on Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms, which support advocacy for the creation 
of an enabling environment for the promotion of gender, civil society organizations and development 
partners, are also part of the system. 
 
1.2. 
Policy and legal framework to reduce Gender Inequalities  
At policy level, there is a Gender National Policy (2008), along with a decadal plan (2009- 2018) for 
the implementation of this policy. Moreover, some sectoral policies also include the promotion of 
gender equality throughout their actions. At a legislative level, the Constitution provides equal rights 
regardless of gender and prohibits sex-based discrimination. Niger has ratified the Convention on the 
Elimination of All forms of Discrimination against Women – CEDAW- in 1999 and has signed the 
Optional Protocol on violence against women (2004). The quota law, enacted since 2000 and which 
enter into force in 2002, define a minimal threshold for the participation to the management of public 
affairs. The law 2004-50, the commercial code and the civil code include also provisions that should 
contribute to more gender equality.  
 
Addressing some of the governance challenges in the public and private sector may bring particular 
benefits to women. Efforts to improve the enabling business environment are likely to be particularly 
important for women, who tend to have less access to the money, time, literacy and social networks 
needed to overcome complex and financially costly/time-consuming regulations. Proxy representation 
funded by development partners could be considered. Efforts to enable the informal sector will also 
benefit women who are even more likely than men to operate informally. If they are done in a gender-
sensitive manner, efforts to improve tenure security can also be especially beneficial for women by 
improving their access to collateral and incentives to make productivity-enhancing investments in land.  
 
There are additional barriers for women trying to conduct business. For instance, the Civil Code, 
provides for the male as the head of the household with ‘marital authority’ over his wife and explicitly 
limits her legal capacity in marriage, including with respect to exercising a profession and opening a 
bank account. Even though the Commercial Code allows women to have independent. 
 
2. Overview of Gender inequalities in Niger 
 
 
2.1. 
Gender analysis in demography level 
As of July 2018, Niger's population was estimated at 21,4 million inhabitants; it is expected to reach 
34.5 million in 2030 and more than triple in 2050 to 69 million. Women account for more than half of 
the population (52.4%), and this share has actually increased in the last seven years according to data 
from the National Statistical Institute. They accounted for just over half (50.3%) of the total population

=== PAGE 138 ===
6 
 
in 2012. Women's fertility rate is one of the highest in Africa, 7.6 children per woman and can reach 9 
in regions such as Maradi and Zinder. The high fertility trends, combined with a rapid decline in child 
mortality, contribute significantly to the very high rate of population growth of 3.9 percent. Some 
cultural aspects drive a preference for children. Some of these are linked to religious interpretations, 
income risks in the face of poverty, and gender discrimination. Without improvements in these general 
conditions (risk, human capital, access to health services for both men and women) and without an 
active (media) engagement to discuss and challenge established norms, population growth is likely to 
remain high. This high fertility rate is likely to have negative impacts on the time than women can 
devote to improve their well-being (better education, health and nutritional status, employment and 
income generation) at the expense of time devoted to child care.

Niger was ranked 189 out of 189 countries in the 2017 Human Development Index (HDI), with a value 
of 0.354. During the period 1990-2017, Niger's HDI value has gone from 0.210 to 0.354, 
corresponding to an increase of 68.5 percent. Based on the sex-disaggregated HDI, a Gender 
Development Index (GDI) is calculated as the ratio of the female to the male HDI to measure gender 
inequalities on three basic dimensions of human development: health (measured by female and male 
life expectancy at birth), education (measured by female and male expected years of schooling for 
children and mean years for adults aged 25 years and older); and command over economic resources 
(measured by female and male estimated GNI per capita). In 2017, the female HDI was 0.317 in Niger 
while the value for male was 0.391, leading therefore to a GDI value of 0.812 
Furthermore, a Gender Inequality Index (GII), is calculated to reflect gender-based inequalities in three 
dimensions related to reproductive health (measured by maternal mortality and adolescent birth rates), 
empowerment (measured by the share of parliamentary seats held by women and attainment in 
secondary and higher education by each gender) and economic activity (measured by the labor 
market participation rate for women and men). Niger is ranked 154 out of 189 countries according to 
UN Annual report, with a GII value of 0.649. The maternal mortality rate is 520 per 100,000 live births 
in 2015 and the adolescent birth rate is 192.0 births per 1,000 women of ages 15-19. Niger has 29 
seats held by women out of 171, representing 16.9 percent. However, the number of women 
parliamentarians has increased compared to 1999 and 2004 with respective rates of 1.2 percent and 
12.4 percent. Regarding representativeness in Government, the share of women is only 18% which is 
far from the target of 25 percent. For local communities, there are 588 local elected officials who are 
women out of a total of 3752, corresponding to a participation rate of 16% which is much lower in 
Zinder, Tahoua and Maradi. Furthermore, 4.3 percent of adult women have reached at least a 
secondary level of education compared to 8.9 percent of their male counterparts. The participation of 
women in the labor market exhibits higher level of gender inequalities with 67.5 percent, compared to 
male participation of 90.7 percent. 
As for the Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), Niger ranks 153rd out of 159 with a SIGI value of 
0.4415 which is very high. This OECD Development Centre’s Index is a cross-country measure of 
discrimination against women in social institutions (formal and informal laws, social norms, and 
practices) across 180 countries. It is worth mentioning that Niger is among a list of 13 Sub-Saharan 
African countries with a high level of gender discrimination based on the SIGI. The table 3 below 
summarizes the above-mentioned rates.

Gender inequalities contribute to poverty which disproportionately affects women, girls and children. 
Given the low ranking of Niger in most of the human development indicators, it is one of the poorest 
countries in the World with a poverty rate of 44.1 percent. Poverty is more important in rural than 
urban areas, and regions like Maradi, Dosso and Zinder are the most affected ones. This is also 
reflected with the Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) for which Niger had a value of 0.605 in 2012; 
and about 89.3 percent of the population is considered as poor according to the MPI. Disparities exist 
between urban and rural areas regarding MPI: rural areas (0.669) which is more than two times higher 
compared to urban areas (0.276). Regions with highest MPI are Maradi (0.664), Tahoua (0.646), 
Zinder (0.641) and Dosso (0.620). High poverty level in rural areas and its effects on people's 
vulnerability gives evidence on the need to create enabling environment for financing climate resilient 
agriculture. 
2.2. Specific gender issues according to the relevant sectors in the project 
Societal norms and expectations limit the full economic potential of women, and by implication of 
society. Women make up more than 70% percent of the Niger ’s workforce in the agricultural sector. 
This sector is critical for economic growth in the poorest country which relies heavily on women. Small 
and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) with female ownership is very limited. When they own 
businesses, they have unmet financial needs and face various barriers to access to assets and 
financing. Access to credit can open up economic opportunities for rural Nigerien women, and bank 
accounts can be a gateway to the use of additional financial services. However, women entrepreneurs 
and employers face significantly greater challenges than men in gaining access to financial services. 
Access to microfinance in Africa remains among the lowest with a penetration rate of less than one 
percent. Key barriers to accessing to financing are gender based social, cultural and legal. To 
transform the agricultural sector under a changing climate, the Government of Niger needs to close 
the Gender Gap in agriculture. 
2.2.1. Employment 
About 34 percent of women are out of the labor force as opposed to 10 percent for men. On average 
active women are employed for fewer hours compared to men (28 for women and 43 for men) while 
receiving lower earnings. In terms of gross national income, it is estimated at US$ 481 per year for

=== PAGE 141 ===
9 
 
women, while for men it is more than two times higher with a value of US$ 1292 PPA. Women who 
are paid are only 16 percent in the private sector and 17% in the public sector; and women led 
companies is very low (5.14 percent). The unemployment rate is estimated at 8.9 percent, with 9.2 
percent for men against 8.1 percent for women. Thus, women still lack behind in terms employment 
and income generation. 
In Niger, more than half of the population is under 15 years old (51,9%), two Nigeriens out of three are 
less than 25 years old (66%) and 33% of Nigeriens are between 15 and 35 years old. Youth 
unemployment is a major problem in Niger, both in urban areas (19.4%) and rural areas (15.1%). In 
rural areas, youth unemployment is explained on the one hand by the lack of productive jobs and on 
the other to the effects of food crises, leading therefore to migration towards cities. Underemployment 
is also an issue in rural areas of Niger. About 34.6% suffer from underemployment, with a rate more 
than two times higher in rural areas (84.6%) because of the seasonality of activities. By 2050, Niger 
dependency ratio will exceed the average for Sub Saharan Africa and other regions (Latin America 
and Asia) by 25 and 32 percent respectively; and the population age between 15-24 will reach 20 
percent, a situation described as the “bulge of youth” 
 
2.2.2. Agriculture 
Gender inequalities also reflect in agricultural productivity. Backiny-Yetna1 and McGee (2015) found 
that plots managed by women in Niger produce 19 percent less per hectare than plots managed by 
men. The main determinants contributing to gender productivity gap in Niger are (a) farm labor, with 
women facing significant challenges in accessing, using, and supervising male farm labor; (b) the 
quantity and quality of fertilizer use, with men using more inorganic fertilizer per hectare than women; 
and (c) land ownership and characteristics, with men owning more land and enjoying higher returns to 
ownership than women. The relatively lower women participation in leadership outside the household, 
as measured by group membership and public speaking, is also considered as a key element that 
affects their agricultural productivity. The low agricultural productivity of women compared to men is 
likely to have impact in their resilience. A study on analysis and measurement of resilience in Niger 
found that female-headed households are less resilient than male-headed households. Female-
headed households made up 16% of the households in Niger according to data from the Demographic 
and Health Survey in 2012. Furthermore, more than one out of three women (36.2 percent) owns at 
least one land plot compared to more than half for men (55.3 percent). Due to gender inequality 
regarding access to productive resources, such as land, and financing, female headed households 
have been found to be poorer and more vulnerable to food and nutrition security. 
2.2.3. Land access  
Whether it is rainfed or irrigated agriculture, access to land through inheritance is the dominant mode 
and is generally reserved for men (36% of women own land in 2012). But access to land ownership is 
possible for both men and women by purchase for a fee. Women are working on AHAs as plot 
owners. The rate of women as plot owner is few. Many of women are generally working as employees. 
The new national approach on AHAs is that women and men have the same right and the equal 
access to land becoming plots owners.  
With the new AHAs, and in this project, land will be voluntarily handed over to ONAHA by landowners 
in a negotiated process. All of this land will then be registered in the name of ONAHA and subdivided 
into 0.125 ha plots. The plot units will be allocated either to a farmer (male, female, young) or to a 
household and its members (headed by a man or a woman). Plot allocations will be made on the basis 
of a farming contract between ONAHA and each beneficiary (man, woman, youth). Women living in 
polygamous unions may, if they wish, be allocated plots under their own names. The rule to access 
 
1 Prospere Backiny-Yetna and Kevin McGee (2015). Gender Differentials and Agricultural Productivity in Niger.  
World Bank Policy Research Working Paper No. 7199

=== PAGE 142 ===
10 
 
the  Hydro agriculture areas’ plots under ONAHA management  is the access equality between men 
and women. 
To improve the achievement of project results, sensitization, information and capacity building 
sessions are planned during the beginning of the project implementation, for men and women. This 
will facilitate their understanding of project actions. Also, a gender expert will be involved in this 
process. finally, it is important to note that the redistribution of plots follows an administrative 
procedure based on the commitment of the Republic of Niger for equality between men and women. 
This practice is aligned to axis 3 of the National Gender Policy (Economic empowerment and inclusive 
growth linked to sustainable management of the environment, climate change, management risks and 
disasters, migration, and humanitarian emergencies). This method of distribution is not influenced by 
traditional practices and promotes equal access to land. 
Also, the traditional system of land management is not favorable to individual property rights. In the 
new form of redistribution of the project, land belong to the state through ONAHA. For individuals, it 
concerns only the right of use or the right of exploitation on the allocated perimeter based on a 
contract signed between ONAHA and each beneficiary. So, this form of access does not meet with 
resistance from the traditional owners or from men. This form promotes more women's access to land. 
In any case, the formulation and the approval of an Environmental and social impact assessment 
(ESIA) is planned for each subproject before the begining of the field works. This ESIA will contain 
grievance redress mechanisms which will be made accessible and operational to both women and 
men at the local, municipal, regional, national and international level. The Terms of reference (ToR) for 
the subprojects ESIA formulation is available at page 130 of the Annex 6: Environmental and social 
management Framework. The grievance redress mechanisms contains proposed in the ToR is at the 
page 132.  
2.2.4. Education 
In rural areas, the literacy rate of women aged 15 years old and higher is very low, particularly in rural 
areas where it was 11 percent in 201410. Long standing traditions attach less value to the education 
of girls than of boys demonstrated by a high propensity of illiterate women compared to men, with 
respective percentages of 75.5 and 67.8. The gender gaps in literacy rates illustrate inequalities in 
enrolment and completion of education, with discrepancies between boys and girls. The primary Gross 
Enrolment Rate (GER) was 77.8 percent in 2017, with 83.3 percent for boys and 72.1 percent for girls. 
This rate for girls has increased over the last four years, from 64.8 percent in 2013 to 72.1 percent in 
2017. The completion rate at primary school also confirms the discrepancies between boys and girls 
regarding their educational status, with 83.3 percent for boys and 72.1 percent for girls while it is 80.3 
percent at national level. These gaps are higher in rural areas compared to urban areas, and 
particularly for regions such as Zinder and Tahoua. This is compounded by the custom of marrying 
girls young (particularly in situations of financial distress and preferably to well-to-do (hence older) 
men from a different climatic zone); prohibiting women from inheriting upon the death of their husband. 
2.3. Gender-Based Violence 
Psychological/emotional abuse, rape, sexual assault, physical assault, forced marriage, and denial of 
resources, opportunities or services are common in Niger. Women are victims of numerous forms of 
violence, especially physical (29%), sexual (20%), early or forced marriage (14%), and lack of access 
to one's own resources (22%).   
According to the "Study on the scope and determinants of gender-based violence in Niger,"2 the 
prevalence rate of all types of violence, regardless of gender, is 53%. The national prevalence of GBV 
is 28.4%. Behind this rate, which is an average, are the rates for children and women, which are much 
higher. In fact, 63% of children in Niger suffer from at least one of the types of violence mentioned 
 
2 Djibo Saley et all, Magnitude and Determinants of Gender-Based Violence in Niger, UNFPA, 2015.

=== PAGE 143 ===
11 
 
above. The prevalence rate among the female population is 60%3. Women are therefore more likely to 
be victims of gender-based violence in Niger. Other studies reveal that the vast majority of 
perpetrators are men (93.4%); they are often married men over 25 years of age, without any level of 
education, related to the victims by marriage in 72% of cases, or are part of the family circle or 
community. The majority of victims are married women under 25 years of age, with no education and 
no source of income. Young girls are not spared either. 
According to the UNHCR, "during the month of December 2019, in the Diffa zone, 36 cases of gender-
based violence were reported and documented. We note 06 cases of rape, including 3 on minors aged 
17, 15 and 14 years. Also, 3 cases of physical violence and 6 cases of psychological violence were 
documented. Similarly, 18 cases of denial of resources and 3 cases of forced marriages were 
recorded, 2 of which involved minors aged 15 and 164. It should be noted that female genital mutilation 
remains a marginal practice in Niger (2%). Young girls in Tillabéry are the most exposed to this 
phenomenon (12%). This practice could be reduced because most men and women consider it to be 
of no benefit5. 
 
2.4. Common gender beliefs, perceptions and stereotypes  
At the customary level, in general, there are social norms that formally or informally regulate the 
respective roles of women and men. Findings from a World Bank study in this area (World Bank, 
2014) reveal that social norm function as rules and can be gender and context specific. These social 
norms are core cultural values of a group or society and shape everyday conduct in private and public 
life. These norms are rarely challenged to avoid disrespecting the group. In effect, social norms define 
the roles assigned to men and women and to which they are expected to conform. For example, in 
some regions and traditions, rural women are obliged not to plant millet or peanuts. In other cases, 
these norms have the effect of limiting women's access to agricultural resources by stating that it is 
socially unacceptable for women to inherit land or engage in agricultural activities. 
The few women who attempt to access land in defiance of convention usually pay the price. They are 
strongly criticized for breaking customs and even accused of being a disgrace to their families. 
According to the normative role, men are the breadwinners, the decision makers. As for women, they 
depend on them and must be under the protection of men. 
In customary law, property rights and rules of intergenerational transmission are still governed by 
gender and enshrine the pre-eminence of men. These realities provide insight into gender inequalities 
attributed to social and customary norms, even though some norms are often unfairly applied or lack a 
genuine traditional basis. 
In terms of beliefs, the two main religions are Islam and Christianity, the first concerns 98% of the 
population and the second about 1%. Beyond spiritual beliefs, religions are practices that condition 
existence, lifestyles, behaviors, family and societal relationships, especially those between fathers and 
children or between spouses. In this configuration, customs, cultures and traditions are intertwined 
with certain rules of life dictated by religions. 
3. Vulnerability to climate change  
Analysis of vulnerability to food insecurity by gender of household head in rural areas shows that 
people living in female-headed households are the most vulnerable to food insecurity related to the 
adverse effects of climate change. Female-headed households are more food insecure than male-
headed households. There are 8.7% of female-headed households versus 2.7% of male-headed 
 
3 National strategy for the prevention and response to gender-based violence (GBV) in Niger (2017-2021), Ministry of Women's Promotion 
and Child Protection, 2017. 
4 UNHCR, "Situation of sexual and gender-based violence - Diffa, Niger," 2019. 
5 Beijing Plus 20 Review Report for Niger, June 2014, Ministry of Population, Women's Promotion and Child Protection

=== PAGE 144 ===
12 
 
households that are severely food insecure and 17.5% versus 9.7% that are moderately food 
insecure. The proportion of food secure households is 41.7% for female-headed households versus 
58.8% for male-headed households. 
In addition, there are gender inequalities that could be exacerbated by climate change impacts in the 
project watershed. These inequalities include unequal access to irrigation water, credit, and income 
levels. Men are more adaptable to climate change than women. Because men and women do not 
have equal access to water resources, these inequalities could be exacerbated by the adverse effects 
of climate change, particularly in the event of prolonged water shortages. 
4. Information and communication  
Gender inequalities in the agricultural sector are not only limited to access to land, but also to the 
personnel who are supposed to conduct policies, transmit the message and supervise the agricultural 
population; this has consequences on the appropriation of information and the level of adherence of 
women in a context where direct exchanges between men and women are often subject to social 
pressures. 
A communication strategy, aimed at both women and men beneficiaries, will be implemented at the 
local level. This process aims to improve their understanding of the adverse effects of climate change 
and to make them aware of opportunities for their practical participation in the development and 
implementation of appropriate technical and technological innovations to adapt to the adverse effects 
of climate change. In order to better manage the gender and conflict sensitivity dimension, existing 
traditional mechanisms will be strengthened with the participation of formal and/or informal community 
structures, traditional communicators, through the support and guidance of local authorities. 
5. Issues related to women's participation in the project 
The many disparities that exist between men and women, particularly in the areas of education, 
vulnerability to climate change, income, access to resources (especially water and land), decision-
making, etc., represent constraints that can limit the full participation of women in project activities. 
Women may, in fact, be discriminated against not only in the distribution of developed plots but also in 
other services and facilities offered by the project (training, capacity building, access to inputs, etc.). 
The following table presents the measures to be implemented during project implementation to 
facilitate and encourage women's participation in the various project activities.

6. Summary of Gender Action Plan 
 
6.1. 
Overview of the Gender Action Plan 
 
6.1.1. General Objective 
Enhance men and women’s equal participation in the project through the improvement and 
diversification of the livelihoods of 121 615 people (including 50% women and girls) in rural 
communities currently dependent on rain-fed agriculture in 5 regions of Niger 
6.1.2. Gender Mainstreaming Results 
Component 1. Sustainable Development and Rehabilitation of Areas  Vulnerable to the 
Negative  effects of Climate Change and Reducing GHG Emissions 
R1 : The capacities of vulnerable groups including women in terms of gender mainstreaming in 
the project are strengthened 
o 
Train women and young producers (farmers) regarding agricultural techniques that are 
resilient to climate change 
o 
Train women and young people in associative life in order to be elected within the governing 
structures of these rural and agricultural organizations 
o 
Strengthen the capacities of women and young people in associative and organizational life 
R2: The technical and organizational capacities of vulnerable actors including 50% of women 
are strengthened 
o 
Build the capacities of actors from the technical support services of the Ministries involved in 
the gender equality project to meet the differentiated needs of men, women and young people 
o 
Train the producers and young producers benefiting from the project in reading and 
interpreting agro-meteorological data 
o 
Strengthen the capacities of men and women of local financial institutions to include climate-
smart agriculture into their activities  
o 
Train project beneficiaries including women and young people in reading and interpreting 
temperature and precipitation data 
o 
Strengthen the capacities of rural women and men and young people in the use of Tools for 
monitoring changes in natural resources 
Component 2 : Support of the sustainable increase in agricultural productivity and income of 
farmers’ groups and cooperatives in irrigated areas on developed and rehabilitated areas 
R3: Women and men have equal access to decision-making bodies 
o 
Make women and young people participants in decision-making relating to project activities 
o 
Choose competent women as members of the teams responsible for the land registration 
process at each site 
o 
Build women, men and young people’s capacity to improve the functionality of their 
organizations so that they are taken into account in the contracts to be signed with 
cooperatives and Water Users Associations (AUEi)  
o 
Elect or choose women and men  to run the committees responsible for the process 
R4 : Women and men have equal access to agricultural and climate-resilient technologies 
o 
Facilitate the access of women heads of household to land and agricultural technologies

17 
 
o 
Facilitate the access of vulnerable groups (women and men) to small agricultural equipment

contribution of women in the planning of activities at the site level

Ensure that gender is taken into account within the teams responsible for capitalizing on

Ensure a gender balance for knowledge sharing and dissemination of good practices for

6.2. 
Introduction to the Gender Action Plan Logframe 
the logical framework below summarizes the objectives, the results to be achieved in order to ensure 
the integration of equality and gender equity in all the components of the project. 
For all activities, baseline data on the number of women/men will be more precised at the 
implementation stage, specifically prior to the project implementation. This specific gender analysis will 
be carried out in each project intervention site. The aim is to identify women heads of households who 
are vulnerable because they do not own land and agricultural equipment that is resilient to climate 
change. In addition, a gender budgeting analysis will be carried out in order to be able to provide 
training to the appropriate stakeholders. In addition, making women and young people partners who

=== PAGE 150 ===
18 
 
participate in decision-making relating to project activities requires their participation in activities 
planning and budget allocation sessions. 
Before the starting of activities, during planning sessions with partners at the local level, there will be a 
definition of vulnerable groups for a common understanding; and especially the choice with which to 
work. 
In training level, due to women’s illiteracy rate, local languages will be used during 
sensitization/awareness campaigns for project beneficiaries. The educated women will explain the 
technical data and information of the project to those who have never attended school and will also 
help them fill out all the documents necessary for their participation in the project. 
Finally, a gender expert will be recruited and integrated to the Project Management Unit (PMU) and 
will be responsible for supporting the trainers, collecting, in the municipalities, data disaggregated by 
sex and age, preparing training and awareness documents on gender mainstreaming. The work will 
be in accordance with the ministries in charge of gender issues in the implementation of the project. 
The budget is stated in Euro.
